Official seal used by the college and the university

**Yale University** is a [private](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_university) [Ivy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivy_League) [research university](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_university) in [New Haven, Connecticut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Haven,_Connecticut), United States. Founded in 1701, Yale is the [third-oldest institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Colonial_Colleges) of [higher education in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education_in_the_United_States). It is one of the nine [colonial colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_colleges) chartered before the [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution).[^cite_note-10]

Yale was established as the **Collegiate School** in 1701 by [Congregationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregationalism_in_the_United_States) clergy of the [Connecticut Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_Colony). Originally focused on theology and [sacred languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_language), the school's curriculum expanded to include humanities and sciences by the time of the American Revolution. In the 19th century, the college expanded to include graduate and professional instruction, awarding the first [PhD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Philosophy) in the United States in 1861 and organizing as a university in 1887. It is one of the founding members of the [Association of American Universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_American_Universities). Yale's faculty and student populations grew rapidly after 1890 due to the expansion of the physical campus and its scientific research programs.[^cite_note-11]

Yale is organized into fifteen constituent schools, including the original [undergraduate college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_College), the [Yale Graduate School of Arts and Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Graduate_School_of_Arts_and_Sciences), and [Yale Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Law_School).[^cite_note-12] While the university is governed by the [Yale Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Corporation), each school's [faculty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_personnel) oversees its curriculum and degree programs. In addition to a central campus in [downtown New Haven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_New_Haven), the university owns athletic facilities in western New Haven, a campus in [West Haven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Haven,_Connecticut), and forests and nature preserves throughout [New England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England). As of 2025, [Yale's endowment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_University_endowment) was valued at \$44.1 billion, making it the [second-largest among all educational institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lists_of_institutions_of_higher_education_by_endowment_size) and the second-largest among [private universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_university).[^cite_note-Endowment-4] The [Yale University Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_University_Library), serving all constituent schools, holds more than 15 million volumes and is the third-largest academic library in the United States.[^cite_note-2013LibraryReport-13][^cite_note-14] Student athletes compete in intercollegiate sports as the [Yale Bulldogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Bulldogs) in the [NCAA](60706.md) [Division I](669931.md) [Ivy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivy_League) conference.

As of October 2025, 72 [Nobel laureates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Nobel_laureates_by_university_affiliation),[^cite_note-15] 5 [Fields medalists](10859.md), 4 [Abel Prize laureates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Prize), and 3 [Turing Award](31093.md) winners have been affiliated with Yale University. Yale alumni include 5 [U.S. presidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States), 10 [Founding Fathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States), 19 U.S. [Supreme Court justices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_justices_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States), 31 living billionaires, 54 college founders and presidents, as well as numerous heads of state, cabinet members, and governors. The university’s community also includes hundreds of members of Congress, U.S. diplomats, 99 [MacArthur Fellows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacArthur_Fellows_Program), 263 [Rhodes Scholars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhodes_Scholarship), 123 [Marshall Scholars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Scholarship), 81 [Gates Cambridge Scholars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gates_Cambridge_Scholarship), 102 [Guggenheim Fellows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guggenheim_Fellowship), and 9 [Mitchell Scholars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitchell_Scholarship). Current Yale faculty include 73 members of the [National Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Sciences), 55 members of the [National Academy of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Medicine), 8 members of the [National Academy of Engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Engineering), and 200 members of the [American Academy of Arts and Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Academy_of_Arts_and_Sciences). The university is also one of the largest producers of [Oscar](324.md), [Emmy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmy_Awards), and [Grammy](44635.md) winners.[^cite_note-16][^cite_note-17] Over 200 Yale alumni[^cite_note-18] have competed in the Olympic games and have won over 110 medals (including 55 gold).[^cite_note-news.yale.edu-19]

## History

### Early history of Yale College

#### Origins

Yale traces its beginnings to "An Act for Liberty to Erect a Collegiate School", a would-be [charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter) passed in [New Haven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Haven) by the General Court of the [Colony of Connecticut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_Colony) on October 9, 1701. The act aimed to establish an institution for the education of ministers and lay leaders. Soon after, a group of ten [Congregational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregationalism_in_the_United_States) ministers, [Samuel Andrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Andrew), Thomas Buckingham, Israel Chauncy, Samuel Mather (nephew of [Increase Mather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Increase_Mather)), the Rev. James Noyes II (son of [James Noyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Noyes)), [James Pierpont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Pierpont_\(Yale_founder\)), [Abraham Pierson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Pierson), [Noadiah Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noadiah_Russell_\(Yale_founder\)), Joseph Webb, and [Timothy Woodbridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timothy_Woodbridge), all [Harvard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard) [alumni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alumni), met in the study of the Reverend [Samuel Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Russell_\(Yale_co-founder\)), in [Branford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Branford,_Connecticut), to donate books to form the school's library.[^cite_note-20] The group, led by [James Pierpont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Pierpont_\(Yale_founder\)), is now known as the "Founders".[^cite_note-21]



Front view of "Yale-College" and the chapel, printed by Daniel Bowen in 1786

Known from its origin as the "Collegiate School", the institution opened in the home of its first [rector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rector_\(academia\)), Abraham Pierson, who is considered Yale's first president. Pierson lived in [Killingworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killingworth,_Connecticut). In 1702, [Nathaniel Chauncey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathaniel_Chauncey) became the first person to attain a degree, having it conferred solely by oral examination by Pierson and Russell.[^cite_note-\:0-22] The school moved to [Saybrook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Saybrook,_Connecticut) in 1707 after Pierson's death, when the first treasurer of Yale, Nathaniel Lynde, donated land and a building.[^cite_note-\:1-23][^cite_note-24] Although the deed required the school to remain in Saybrook, the trustees voted in 1716 to move it to New Haven, which had outbid several other communities for the right to host it.[^cite_note-25]

Meanwhile, there was a rift forming at Harvard between its sixth president, Increase Mather, and the rest of the Harvard clergy, whom Mather viewed as increasingly liberal, ecclesiastically lax, and overly broad in [Church polity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiastical_polity). The feud caused the Mathers to champion the Collegiate School in the hope it would maintain the [Puritan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puritan) religious orthodoxy in a way Harvard had not.[^cite_note-26] The Reverend [Jason Haven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_Haven), minister at the [First Church and Parish in Dedham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Church_and_Parish_in_Dedham), Massachusetts, had been considered for the presidency on account of his orthodox theology and "Neatness dignity and purity of Style \[which] surpass those of all that have been mentioned", but was passed over due to his "very Valetudinary and infirm State of Health".[^cite_note-27]

#### Naming and development



Coat of arms of the family of [Elihu Yale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elihu_Yale), for whom the university was named in 1718

In 1718, at the behest of either Rector [Samuel Andrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Andrew) or the colony's Governor [Gurdon Saltonstall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurdon_Saltonstall), [Cotton Mather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton_Mather) contacted the Boston-born businessman [Elihu Yale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elihu_Yale) to ask for money to construct a new building for the college. Through the persuasion of [Jeremiah Dummer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremiah_Dummer), Yale, who had made a fortune in [Madras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chennai) while working for the [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company) as the first president of [Fort St. George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_St._George), donated nine bales of goods, which were sold for more than £560, a substantial sum of money. Cotton Mather suggested the school change its name to "Yale College".[^cite_note-28]

Meanwhile, a Harvard graduate working in England convinced 180 prominent intellectuals to donate books to Yale. The 1714 shipment of 500 books represented the best of modern English literature, science, philosophy and theology.[^cite_note-29] It had a profound effect on intellectuals at Yale. Undergraduate [Jonathan Edwards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Edwards_\(theologian\)) discovered [John Locke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Locke)'s works and developed his "[new divinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_divinity)". In 1722, the rector and six friends, who had a study group to discuss the new ideas, announced they had given up [Calvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvinism), become [Arminians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arminianism), and joined the [Church of England](5955.md). They were ordained in England and returned to the colonies as missionaries for the [Anglican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican) faith. [Thomas Clapp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Clapp) became president in 1745, and while he attempted to return the college to Calvinist orthodoxy, did not close the library. Other students found [Deist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deist) books in the library.[^cite_note-30]

#### Curriculum



Connecticut Hall, the oldest building on the Yale campus, built between 1750 and 1753



The first diploma awarded by [Yale College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_College), granted to Nathaniel Chauncey in 1702

Yale College undergraduates follow a [liberal arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_arts_education) curriculum with departmental [majors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_\(academic\)) and is organized into a social system of [residential colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Residential_colleges_of_Yale_University).

Yale was swept up by the great intellectual movements of the period—the [Great Awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Awakening) and [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment)—due to the religious and scientific interests of presidents [Thomas Clap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Clap) and [Ezra Stiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ezra_Stiles). They were instrumental in developing the scientific curriculum while dealing with wars, student tumults, graffiti, "irrelevance" of curricula, desperate need for endowment and disagreements with the [Connecticut legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_General_Assembly).[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32]

Serious American students of theology and divinity, particularly in New England, regarded [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language) as a [classical language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_language), along with Greek and [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin), and essential for study of the [Hebrew Bible (Tanakh)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible) in the original. The Reverend Mr. Stiles, president from 1778 to 1795, brought with him his interest in Hebrew as a vehicle for studying ancient [Biblical texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible) in their original language, requiring all freshmen to study Hebrew (in contrast to Harvard, where only upperclassmen were required to study it) and is responsible for the Hebrew phrase אורים ותמים ([Urim and Thummim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urim_and_Thummim)) on the Yale seal. A 1746 graduate of Yale, Stiles came to the college with experience in education, having played an integral role in founding [Brown University](4157.md).[^cite_note-33] Stiles' greatest challenge occurred in 1779 when British forces occupied New Haven and threatened to raze the college. However, Yale graduate [Edmund Fanning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Fanning_\(colonial_administrator\)), secretary to the British general in command of the occupation, intervened and the college was saved. In 1803, Fanning was granted an honorary degree [LL.D.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LL.D.)[^cite_note-34]

#### Students

As the only college in Connecticut from 1701 to 1823, Yale educated the sons of the elite.[^cite_note-35] Punishable offenses included [cardplaying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardplaying), tavern-going, destruction of college property, and acts of disobedience. Harvard was distinctive for the stability and maturity of its tutor corps, while Yale had youth and zeal.[^cite_note-36]

The emphasis on classics gave rise to private student societies, open only by invitation, which arose as forums for discussions of scholarship, literature and politics. The first were debating societies: [Crotonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crotonia_\(literary_society\)) in 1738, [Linonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linonia) in 1753 and [Brothers in Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brothers_in_Unity) in 1768. Linonia and Brothers in Unity continue to exist; commemorations to them can be found with names given to campus structures, like Brothers in Unity Courtyard in Branford College.

### 19th century



Old Brick Row in 1807

The [Yale Report of 1828](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Report_of_1828) was a dogmatic defense of the Latin and Greek curriculum against critics who wanted more courses in modern languages, math and science. Unlike [higher education in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education_in_Europe), there was no [national curriculum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_curriculum) for U.S. colleges and universities. In the competition for students and financial support, college leaders strove to keep current with demands for innovation. At the same time, they realized a significant portion of students and prospective students demanded a classical background. The report meant the classics would not be abandoned. During this period, institutions experimented with changes in the curriculum, often resulting in a dual-track curriculum. In the decentralized environment of U.S. higher education, balancing change with tradition was a common challenge.[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38] A group of professors at Yale and New Haven Congregationalist ministers articulated a conservative response to the changes brought by [Victorian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_culture). They concentrated on developing a person possessed of religious values strong enough to sufficiently resist temptations from within, yet flexible enough to adjust to the "[isms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/-ism)" ([professionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professionalism), [materialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Materialism), [individualism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individualism), and [consumerism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumerism)) tempting them from without.[^cite_note-39] [William Graham Sumner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Graham_Sumner), professor from 1872 to 1909, taught in the emerging disciplines of economics and sociology to overflowing classrooms. Sumner bested President [Noah Porter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noah_Porter), who disliked the social sciences and wanted Yale to lock into its traditions of classical education. Porter objected to Sumner's use of a textbook by [Herbert Spencer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Spencer) that espoused agnostic materialism because it might harm students.[^cite_note-40]

Until 1887, the legal name of the university was "The President and Fellows of Yale College, in New Haven". In 1887, under an act passed by the [Connecticut General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_General_Assembly), Yale was renamed "Yale University".[^cite_note-41]

#### Sports and debate

The Revolutionary War soldier [Nathan Hale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathan_Hale) (Yale 1773) was the archetype of the Yale ideal in the early 19th century: a manly yet aristocratic scholar, well-versed in knowledge and sports, and a patriot who "regretted" that he "had but one life to lose" for his country. Western painter [Frederic Remington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederic_Remington) (Yale 1900) was an artist whose heroes gloried in the combat and tests of strength in the Wild West. The fictional, turn-of-the-20th-century Yale man [Frank Merriwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Merriwell) embodied this same heroic ideal without racial prejudice, and his fictional successor Dink Stover in the novel *[Stover at Yale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stover_at_Yale)* (1912) questioned the business mentality that had become prevalent at the school. Increasingly students turned to athletic stars as their heroes, especially since winning the big game became the goal of the student body, the alumni, and the team itself.[^cite_note-42]



Yale's four-oared [crew](25736.md) team posing with the 1876 Centennial [Regatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regatta) trophy, won in [Philadelphia, Pennsylvania](50585.md)

Along with [Harvard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard) and [Princeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_University), Yale students rejected British concepts about '[amateurism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amateurism)' and constructed athletic programs that were uniquely American.[^cite_note-43] The [Harvard–Yale football rivalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard%E2%80%93Yale_football_rivalry) began in 1875. Between 1892, when Harvard and Yale met in one of the first [intercollegiate debates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercollegiate_debates),[^cite_note-44] and in 1909 (year of the first Triangular Debate of Harvard/Yale/Princeton) the rhetoric, symbolism, and metaphors used in athletics were used to frame these debates. Debates were covered on front pages of [college newspapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_newspaper) and emphasized in [yearbooks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yearbook), and team members received the equivalent of [athletic letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varsity_letter) for their jackets. There were rallies to send off teams to matches, but they never attained the broad appeal athletics enjoyed. One reason may be that debates do not have a clear winner, because scoring is subjective. With late 19th-century concerns about the impact of modern life on the body, athletics offered hope that neither the individual nor society was coming apart.[^cite_note-45]

In 1909–10, football faced a crisis resulting from the failure of the reforms of 1905–06, which sought to solve the problem of serious injuries. There was a mood of alarm and mistrust, and, while the crisis was developing, the presidents of Harvard, Yale, and Princeton developed a project to reform the sport and forestall possible radical changes forced by government. Presidents [Arthur Hadley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Twining_Hadley) of Yale, [A. Lawrence Lowell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._Lawrence_Lowell) of Harvard, and [Woodrow Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson) of Princeton worked to develop moderate reforms to reduce injuries. Their attempts, however, were reduced by rebellion against the rules committee and formation of the [Intercollegiate Athletic Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercollegiate_Athletic_Association). While the big three had attempted to operate independently of the majority, the changes pushed did reduce injuries.[^cite_note-46]

#### Expansion

Starting with the addition of the [Yale School of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Medicine) in 1810, the college expanded gradually, establishing the [Yale Divinity School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Divinity_School) in 1822, [Yale Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Law_School) in 1822, the [Yale Graduate School of Arts and Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Graduate_School_of_Arts_and_Sciences) in 1847, the now-defunct [Sheffield Scientific School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheffield_Scientific_School) in 1847,[^cite_note-NoteA-47] and the [Yale School of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Art) in 1869. In 1887, under the presidency of [Timothy Dwight V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timothy_Dwight_V), Yale College was renamed to Yale University, and the former name was applied only to the [undergraduate college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_College). The university continued to expand into the 20th and 21st centuries, adding the [Yale School of Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Music) in 1894, the [Yale School of Forestry & Environmental Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Forestry_&_Environmental_Studies) in 1900, the [Yale School of Public Health](8017475.md) in 1915, the [Yale School of Architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Architecture) in 1916, the [Yale School of Nursing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Nursing) 1923, the [Yale School of Drama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Drama) in 1955, the [Yale School of Management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Management) in 1976, and the [Jackson School of Global Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson_School_of_Global_Affairs) in 2022.[^cite_note-The_Future_of_Jackson-48] The Sheffield Scientific School would also reorganize its relationship with the university to teach only undergraduate courses.

Expansion caused controversy about Yale's new roles. [Noah Porter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noah_Porter), a moral philosopher, was president from 1871 to 1886. During an age of expansion in higher education, Porter resisted the rise of the new research university, claiming an eager embrace of its ideals would corrupt undergraduate education. Historian George Levesque argues Porter was not a simple-minded reactionary, uncritically committed to tradition, but a principled and selective conservative.[^cite_note-49] Levesque says he did not endorse everything old or reject everything new; rather, he sought to apply long-established ethical and pedagogical principles to a changing culture. Levesque concludes, noting he may have misunderstood some of the challenges, but he correctly anticipated the enduring tensions that have accompanied the emergence of the modern university.

### 20th century

#### Medicine



[Woolsey Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woolsey_Hall) c. 1905

Milton Winternitz led the [Yale School of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Medicine) as its dean from 1920 to 1935. Dedicated to the new scientific medicine established in Germany, he was equally fervent about "social medicine" and the study of humans in their environment. He established the "Yale System" of teaching, with few lectures and fewer exams, and strengthened the full-time faculty system; he created the graduate-level Yale School of Nursing and the psychiatry department and built new buildings. Progress toward his plans for an Institute of Human Relations, envisioned as a refuge where social scientists would collaborate with biological scientists in a holistic study of humankind, lasted only a few years before resentful antisemitic colleagues drove him to resign.[^cite_note-50]

#### Faculty



Richard Rummell's 1906 watercolor of the Yale campus facing north

Before [World War II](32927.md), most elite university faculties counted among their numbers few, if any, Jews, black people, women, or other minorities; Yale was no exception. By 1980, this condition had been altered dramatically, as numerous members of those groups held faculty positions.[^cite_note-51] Almost all members of the Faculty of Arts and Sciences—and some members of other faculties—teach undergraduate courses, more than 2,000 of which are offered annually.[^cite_note-52]

#### Women

In 1793, [Lucinda Foote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucinda_Foote) passed the entrance exams for Yale College, but was rejected by the president on the basis of her gender.[^cite_note-bookof-53] Women studied at Yale from 1892, in graduate-level programs at the [Yale Graduate School of Arts and Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Graduate_School_of_Arts_and_Sciences).[^cite_note-\:1-23] The first seven women to earn PhDs received their degrees in 1894: [Elizabeth Deering Hanscom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Deering_Hanscom), Cornelia H. B. Rogers, Sara Bulkley Rogers, [Margaretta Palmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaretta_Palmer), [Mary Augusta Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Augusta_Scott), Laura Johnson Wylie, and [Charlotte Fitch Roberts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlotte_Fitch_Roberts). There is a portrait of them in [Sterling Memorial Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sterling_Memorial_Library), painted by [Brenda Zlamany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brenda_Zlamany).[^cite_note-54]

In 1966, Yale began discussions with its [sister school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sister_school) [Vassar College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vassar_College) about merging to foster coeducation at the undergraduate level. Vassar, then all-female and part of the [Seven Sisters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Sisters_\(colleges\))—elite higher education schools that served as sister institutions to the [Ivy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivy_League) when nearly all Ivy League institutions still only admitted men—tentatively accepted, but then declined the invitation. Both schools introduced coeducation independently in 1969.[^cite_note-55] Amy Solomon was the first woman to register as a Yale undergraduate;[^cite_note-56] she was the first woman at Yale to join an undergraduate society, [St. Anthony Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Anthony_Hall). The undergraduate class of 1973 was the first to have women starting from freshman year;[^cite_note-57] all undergraduate women were housed in Vanderbilt Hall.[^cite_note-58]

A decade into co-education, student assault and harassment by faculty became the impetus for the trailblazing lawsuit *[Alexander v. Yale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_v._Yale)*. In the 1970s, a group of students and a faculty member sued Yale for its failure to curtail sexual harassment, especially by male faculty. The case was partly built from a 1977 report authored by plaintiff [Ann Olivarius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ann_Olivarius), "A report to the Yale Corporation from the Yale Undergraduate Women's Caucus".[^cite_note-59] This case was the first to use [Title IX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Title_IX) to argue and establish that sexual harassment of female students can be considered illegal sex discrimination. The plaintiffs were Olivarius, Ronni Alexander, Margery Reifler, Pamela Price,[^cite_note-60] and Lisa E. Stone. They were joined by Yale classics professor [John J. Winkler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_J._Winkler). The lawsuit, brought partly by [Catharine MacKinnon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catharine_A._MacKinnon), alleged rape, fondling, and offers of higher grades for sex by faculty, including [Keith Brion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keith_Brion), professor of flute and director of bands, political science professor Raymond Duvall,[^cite_note-61] English professor [Michael Cooke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_George_Cooke), and coach of the field hockey team, Richard Kentwell. While unsuccessful in the courts, the legal reasoning changed the landscape of sex discrimination law and resulted in the establishment of Yale's Grievance Board and Women's Center.[^cite_note-62] In 2011 a Title IX complaint was filed against Yale by students and graduates, including editors of Yale's feminist magazine *[Broad Recognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broad_Recognition)*, alleging the university had a hostile sexual climate.[^cite_note-63] In response, the university formed a Title IX steering committee to address complaints of sexual misconduct.[^cite_note-64] Afterwards, universities and colleges throughout the U.S. also established sexual harassment grievance procedures.

#### Social class

Yale instituted policies in the early 20th century designed to maintain the proportion of [white Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Anglo-Saxon_Protestants#Education) from notable families in the student body (see *[numerus clausus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numerus_clausus)*) and eliminated such preferences, beginning with the class of 1970.[^cite_note-65]

### 21st century

In 2006, Yale and [Peking University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peking_University) (PKU) established a joint undergraduate program in Beijing, an exchange program allowing Yale students to spend a semester living and studying with PKU honor students.[^cite_note-Gideon-2012-66] In July 2012, the Yale University-PKU Program ended due to weak participation.[^cite_note-Gideon-2012-66]

In 2007 outgoing Yale President [Rick Levin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rick_Levin) characterized Yale's institutional priorities: "First, among the nation's finest research universities, Yale is distinctively committed to excellence in undergraduate education. Second, in our graduate and professional schools, as well as in Yale College, we are committed to the education of leaders."[^cite_note-67]

Also in 2007, the university purchased the 137-acre (0.55 km<sup>2</sup>) former [Bayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayer) campus in [West Haven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Haven,_Connecticut) and [Orange, Connecticut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange,_Connecticut), including 17 buildings. The Yale West Campus focuses on [biotechnology](4502.md), [pharmaceuticals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceutical) and other life sciences research.[^cite_note-68]

In 2009, former [British Prime Minister](24150.md) [Tony Blair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Blair) picked Yale as one location—the others being Britain's [Durham University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durham_University) and [Universiti Teknologi Mara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universiti_Teknologi_Mara)—for the [Tony Blair Faith Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Blair_Faith_Foundation)'s United States Faith and Globalization Initiative.[^cite_note-69] As of 2009, former Mexican President [Ernesto Zedillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernesto_Zedillo) was the director of the [Yale Center for the Study of Globalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Center_for_the_Study_of_Globalization) and teaches an undergraduate seminar, "Debating Globalization".[^cite_note-70] As of 2009, former presidential candidate and DNC chair [Howard Dean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Dean) taught a residential college seminar, "Understanding Politics and Politicians".[^cite_note-71] Also in 2009, an alliance was formed among Yale, [University College London](52029.md), and both schools' affiliated hospital complexes to conduct research focused on the direct improvement of patient care—a field known as [translational medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Translational_medicine). President Richard Levin noted that Yale has hundreds of other partnerships across the world, but "no existing collaboration matches the scale of the new partnership with UCL".[^cite_note-72] In August 2013, a new partnership with the [National University of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_University_of_Singapore) led to the opening of [Yale-NUS College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale-NUS_College) in Singapore, a joint effort to create a new liberal arts college in Asia featuring a curriculum including Western and Asian traditions.[^cite_note-73]

In 2017, having been suggested for decades,[^cite_note-74] Yale University renamed Calhoun College, named for [slave owner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States), [anti-abolitionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-abolitionist), and [white supremacist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_supremacist) Vice President [John C. Calhoun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_C._Calhoun). It is now Hopper College, after [Grace Hopper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grace_Hopper).[^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76]

In 2019, Yale was one of several universities involved in the [2019 college admissions bribery scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_college_admissions_bribery_scandal).[^cite_note-77][^cite_note-78]

In 2020, in the wake of the [George Floyd protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Floyd_protests), the #CancelYale tag was used on social media to demand that Elihu Yale's name be removed from Yale University. Much of the support originated from right-wing pundits such as [Mike Cernovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Cernovich) and [Ann Coulter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ann_Coulter), who intended to satirize what they perceived as the excesses of [cancel culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cancel_culture).[^cite_note-79] Yale spent most of his professional career in the employ of the [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company) (EIC), serving as the [governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colonial_governors_and_presidents_of_Madras_Presidency) of the [Presidency of Fort St. George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madras_Presidency) in modern-day [Chennai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chennai). The EIC, including Yale himself, was involved in the [Indian Ocean slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean_slave_trade), though the extent of Yale's involvement in slavery remains debated.[^cite_note-80] His singularly large donation led critics to argue Yale University relied on money derived from slavery for its first scholarships and endowments.[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-Digital_Histories_At_Yale-82][^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84]

In 2020, the U.S. [Justice Department](52563.md) sued Yale for alleged discrimination against Asian and white candidates, through affirmative action admission policies.[^cite_note-85] In 2021, under the new Biden administration, the Justice Department withdrew the lawsuit. The group, [Students for Fair Admissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Students_for_Fair_Admissions), later won a similar [lawsuit against Harvard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Students_for_Fair_Admissions_v._President_and_Fellows_of_Harvard_College).[^cite_note-86]

In April 2024, Yale students joined [other campuses across the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_pro-Palestinian_protests_on_university_campuses_in_the_United_States_in_2024) in protests against the [Gaza war](74998519.md).[^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88] The student protestors demanded that Yale University [divest from military weapons companies with ties to Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boycott,_Divestment_and_Sanctions)'s war on Gaza.[^cite_note-89] Over 50 people were arrested at protests in and around [Beinecke Plaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hewitt_Quadrangle), and protests continued during the summer and in the new academic year starting September 2024.[^cite_note-90] Undergraduate students "overwhelmingly" voted in a December referendum to call for divestment.[^cite_note-91]

In July 2025 Russian authorities declared Yale University to be an ["undesirable" organization](47211692.md), banning its activities in the country. According to the [Russian Prosecutor General’s Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosecutor-General_of_Russia), the institution’s activities are aimed at "violating the territorial integrity of Russia" and "destabilizing the socio-economic and political situation".[^cite_note-bloomberg-20250708-92]

#### Alumni in politics

*[The Boston Globe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Boston_Globe)* wrote in 2002 that "if there's one school that can lay claim to educating the nation's top national leaders over the past three decades, it's Yale."[^cite_note-Magazine_p._6-93] [Yale alumni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_alumni) were represented on the [Democratic](5043544.md) or [Republican](32070.md) ticket in every U.S. presidential election between 1972 and 2004.[^cite_note-94] Yale-educated presidents since the end of the [Vietnam War](32611.md) include [Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford), [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush), [Bill Clinton](3356.md), and [George W. Bush](3414021.md), and major-party nominees include [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) (2016), [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry) (2004), [Joseph Lieberman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Lieberman) (vice president, 2000), and [Sargent Shriver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sargent_Shriver) (vice president, 1972). Other alumni who have made serious bids for the presidency include [Amy Klobuchar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Klobuchar) (2020), [Tom Steyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Steyer) (2020), [Ben Carson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Carson) (2016), [Howard Dean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Dean) (2004), [Gary Hart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Hart) (1984 and 1988), [Paul Tsongas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Tsongas) (1992), [Pat Robertson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Robertson) (1988) and [Jerry Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerry_Brown) (1976, 1980, 1992).

Several explanations have been offered for Yale's representation since the end of the Vietnam War. Sources note the spirit of campus activism that has existed at Yale since the 1960s, and the intellectual influence of the Reverend [William Sloane Coffin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Sloane_Coffin) on future candidates.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-95] Yale President Levin attributes the run to Yale's focus on creating "a laboratory for future leaders", an institutional priority that began during the tenure of Yale Presidents [Alfred Whitney Griswold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Whitney_Griswold) and [Kingman Brewster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingman_Brewster).[^cite_note-ReferenceA-95] [Richard H. Brodhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_H._Brodhead), former dean of Yale College and now president of [Duke University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_University), stated: "We do give very significant attention to orientation to the community in our admissions, and there is a very strong tradition of [volunteerism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volunteerism) at Yale."[^cite_note-Magazine_p._6-93] Yale historian [Gaddis Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaddis_Smith) notes "an ethos of organized activity" at Yale during the 20th century that led Kerry to lead the [Yale Political Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Political_Union)'s Liberal Party, [George Pataki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Pataki) the Conservative Party, and Lieberman to manage the *[Yale Daily News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Daily_News)*.[^cite_note-96] [Camille Paglia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camille_Paglia) points to a history of networking and elitism: "It has to do with a web of friendships and affiliations built up in school."[^cite_note-97] CNN suggests that George W. Bush benefited from preferential admissions policies for the "son and grandson of alumni" and for a "member of a politically influential family".[^cite_note-98] [Elisabeth Bumiller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elisabeth_Bumiller) and [James Fallows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Fallows) credit the culture of community that exists between students, faculty, and administration, which downplays self-interest and reinforces commitment to others.[^cite_note-Goldstein-2004-99]

During the 1988 presidential election, [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) (Yale '48) derided [Michael Dukakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Dukakis) for having "foreign-policy views born in Harvard Yard's boutique". When challenged on the distinction between Dukakis's Harvard connection and his Yale background, he said that, unlike Harvard, Yale's reputation was "so diffuse, there isn't a symbol, I don't think, in the Yale situation, any symbolism in it" and said Yale did not share Harvard's reputation for "liberalism and elitism".[^cite_note-100] In 2004 [Howard Dean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Dean) stated, "In some ways, I consider myself separate from the other three (Yale) candidates of 2004. Yale changed so much between the class of '68 and the class of '71. My class was the first class to have women in it; it was the first class to have a significant effort to recruit African Americans. It was an extraordinary time, and in that span of time is the change of an entire generation."[^cite_note-Goldstein-2004-99]

## Administration and organization

### Leadership

The [President and Fellows of Yale College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_and_Fellows_of_Yale_College), also known as the Yale Corporation, or board of trustees, is the governing body of the university and consists of thirteen standing committees with separate responsibilities outlined in the by-laws. The corporation has 19 members: three *[ex officio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ex_officio_member)* members, ten successor trustees, and six elected alumni fellows.[^cite_note-101] The university has three major academic components: Yale College (the undergraduate program), the Graduate School of Arts and Sciences, and the twelve professional schools.[^cite_note-102]

The Yale Provost's Office and similar executive positions have launched several women into prominent university executive positions. In 1977, Provost [Hanna Holborn Gray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanna_Holborn_Gray) was appointed interim president of Yale and later went on to become president of the University of Chicago, being the first woman to hold either position at each respective school.[^cite_note-103][^cite_note-104] In 1994, Provost [Judith Rodin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judith_Rodin) became the first permanent female president of an Ivy League institution at the [University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Pennsylvania).[^cite_note-105] In 2002, Provost [Alison Richard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alison_Richard) became the vice-chancellor of the [University of Cambridge](25978572.md).[^cite_note-106] In 2003, the dean of the Divinity School, [Rebecca Chopp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebecca_Chopp), was appointed president of [Colgate University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colgate_University) and later went on to serve as the president of [Swarthmore College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swarthmore_College) in 2009, and then the first female chancellor of the [University of Denver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Denver) in 2014.[^cite_note-107] In 2004, Provost [Dr. Susan Hockfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_Hockfield) became the president of the [Massachusetts Institute of Technology](18879.md).[^cite_note-108] In 2004, Dean of the Nursing school, Catherine Gilliss, was appointed the dean of Duke University's School of Nursing and vice chancellor for nursing affairs.[^cite_note-109] In 2007, Deputy Provost [H. Kim Bottomly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._Kim_Bottomly) was named president of [Wellesley College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellesley_College).[^cite_note-110]

Similar examples for men who have served in Yale leadership positions can also be found. In 2004, Dean of Yale College [Richard H. Brodhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_H._Brodhead) was appointed as the president of [Duke University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_University).[^cite_note-111] In 2008, Provost [Andrew Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_D._Hamilton) was confirmed to be the vice chancellor of the University of Oxford.[^cite_note-112]

### Staff and labor unions

See also: [Federation of Hospital and University Employees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Hospital_and_University_Employees)

Yale University staff are represented by several different unions. Clerical and technical workers are represented by Local 34, and service and maintenance workers are represented by Local 35, both of the same union affiliate [UNITE HERE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNITE_HERE).[^cite_note-It's_Your_Yale-113] Unlike similar institutions, Yale has consistently refused to recognize its graduate student union, Local 33 (another affiliate of UNITE HERE), citing claims that the union's elections were undemocratic and how graduate students are not employees;[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115] the move to not recognize the union has been criticized by the [American Federation of Teachers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Federation_of_Teachers).[^cite_note-116] In addition, officers of the Yale University Police Department are represented by the Yale Police Benevolent Association, which affiliated in 2005 with the Connecticut Organization for Public Safety Employees.[^cite_note-It's_Your_Yale-113][^cite_note-117] Yale security officers joined the International Union of Security, Police and Fire Professionals of America in late 2010,[^cite_note-118] even though the Yale administration contested the election.[^cite_note-119] In October 2014, after deliberation,[^cite_note-120] Yale security decided to form a new union, the Yale University Security Officers Association, which has since represented the campus security officers.[^cite_note-It's_Your_Yale-113][^cite_note-121]

Yale has a history of difficult and prolonged labor negotiations, often culminating in strikes.[^cite_note-122] There have been at least eight strikes since 1968, and *[The New York Times](30680.md)* wrote that Yale has a reputation as having the worst record of labor tension of any university in the U.S.[^cite_note-123] Moreover, Yale has been accused by the [AFL–CIO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFL%E2%80%93CIO) of failing to treat workers with respect,[^cite_note-124] as well as not renewing contracts with professors over involvement in campus labor issues.[^cite_note-125] Yale has responded to strikes with claims over mediocre union participation and the benefits of their contracts.[^cite_note-126]

## Campus



[Yale Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Law_School), located in the Sterling Law Building



[Interior of Beinecke Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beinecke_Library)

Yale's central campus in [downtown New Haven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_New_Haven) covers 260 acres (1.1 km<sup>2</sup>) and comprises its main, historic campus and a medical campus adjacent to the [Yale–New Haven Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale%E2%80%93New_Haven_Hospital). In western New Haven, the university holds 500 acres (2.0 km<sup>2</sup>) of athletic facilities, including the [Yale Golf Course](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Golf_Course).[^cite_note-127] In 2008, Yale purchased the 17-building, 136-acre (0.55 km<sup>2</sup>) former [Bayer HealthCare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayer) complex in [West Haven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Haven,_Connecticut), Connecticut,[^cite_note-128] the buildings of which are now used as laboratory and research space.[^cite_note-129] Yale also owns seven forests in Connecticut, Vermont, and New Hampshire—the largest of which is the 7,840-acre (31.7 km<sup>2</sup>) [Yale-Myers Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale-Myers_Forest) in Connecticut's [Quiet Corner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quiet_Corner)—and nature preserves including [Horse Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thimble_Islands).[^cite_note-130]

Yale is noted for its largely [Collegiate Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collegiate_Gothic) campus[^cite_note-131] as well as several iconic modern buildings commonly discussed in architectural history survey courses: [Louis Kahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Kahn)'s Yale Art Gallery[^cite_note-132] and Center for British Art, [Eero Saarinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eero_Saarinen)'s Ingalls Rink and Ezra Stiles and Morse Colleges, and [Paul Rudolph's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Rudolph_\(architect\)) [Art & Architecture Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Art_and_Architecture_Building). Yale also owns and has restored many noteworthy 19th-century mansions along [Hillhouse Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillhouse_Avenue), which was considered the most beautiful street in America by [Charles Dickens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Dickens) when he visited the United States in the 1840s.[^cite_note-133] In 2011, *[Travel + Leisure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_+_Leisure)* listed the Yale campus as one of the most beautiful in the United States.[^cite_note-134] [Condé Nast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cond%C3%A9_Nast_Traveler) also ranked it as one of the most beautiful in 2025.[^cite_note-135]

Many of Yale's buildings were constructed in the [Collegiate Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collegiate_Gothic) architecture style from 1917 to 1931, financed largely by [Edward S. Harkness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_S._Harkness), including the [Yale Drama School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Drama_School).[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137] Stone sculpture built into the walls of the buildings portray contemporary college personalities, such as a writer, an athlete, a tea-drinking socialite, and a student who has fallen asleep while reading. Similarly, the decorative [friezes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frieze) on the buildings depict contemporary scenes, like a policemen chasing a robber and arresting a prostitute (on the wall of the Law School), or a student relaxing with a mug of beer and a cigarette. The architect, [James Gamble Rogers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Gamble_Rogers), faux-aged these buildings by splashing the walls with acid,[^cite_note-138] deliberately breaking their [leaded glass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lead_glass) windows and repairing them in the style of the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), and creating niches for decorative statuary but leaving them empty to simulate loss or theft over the ages. In fact, the buildings merely simulate Middle Ages architecture, for though they appear to be constructed of solid stone blocks in the authentic manner, most actually have steel framing as was commonly used in 1930. One exception is [Harkness Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harkness_Tower), 216 feet (66 m) tall, which was originally a free-standing stone structure. It was reinforced in 1964 to allow the installation of the [Yale Memorial Carillon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Memorial_Carillon).



Statue of [Nathan Hale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathan_Hale) in front of [Connecticut Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_Hall)



[Vanderbilt Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanderbilt_family)

Other examples of the Gothic style are on the [Old Campus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Campus) by architects like [Henry Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Austin_\(architect\)), [Charles C. Haight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_C._Haight) and [Russell Sturgis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russell_Sturgis). Several are associated with members of the [Vanderbilt family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanderbilt_family), including Vanderbilt Hall,[^cite_note-139] Phelps Hall,[^cite_note-140] [St. Anthony Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Anthony_Hall) (a commission for member [Frederick William Vanderbilt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_William_Vanderbilt)), the Mason, Sloane and Osborn laboratories, dormitories for the [Sheffield Scientific School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheffield_Scientific_School) (the engineering and sciences school at Yale until 1956) and elements of [Silliman College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silliman_College), the largest residential college.[^cite_note-141]

The oldest building on campus, [Connecticut Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_Hall) (built in 1750), is in the [Georgian style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_architecture). Georgian-style buildings erected from 1929 to 1933 include [Timothy Dwight College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timothy_Dwight_College), [Pierson College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierson_College), and [Davenport College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davenport_College), except the latter's east, York Street façade, which was constructed in the [Gothic style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_architecture) to coordinate with adjacent structures.

The [Beinecke Rare Book and Manuscript Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beinecke_Rare_Book_and_Manuscript_Library), designed by [Gordon Bunshaft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gordon_Bunshaft) of [Skidmore, Owings & Merrill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skidmore,_Owings_&_Merrill), is one of the largest buildings in the world reserved exclusively for the preservation of rare books and manuscripts. The library includes a six-story above-ground tower of book stacks, filled with 180,000 volumes, that is surrounded by large translucent Vermont marble panels and a steel and granite truss. The panels act as windows and subdue direct sunlight while also diffusing the light in warm hues throughout the interior.[^cite_note-142] Near the library is a sunken courtyard with sculptures by [Isamu Noguchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isamu_Noguchi) that are said to represent time (the pyramid), the sun (the circle), and chance (the cube).[^cite_note-143] The library is located near the center of the university in [Hewitt Quadrangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hewitt_Quadrangle), which is now more commonly referred to as "[Beinecke Plaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beinecke_Plaza)".

Alumnus [Eero Saarinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eero_Saarinen), Finnish-American architect of such notable structures as the [Gateway Arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gateway_Arch) in St. Louis, [Dulles International Airport Main Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dulles_International_Airport_Main_Terminal), [Bell Labs Holmdel Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bell_Labs_Holmdel_Complex) and the [CBS Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS_Building) in Manhattan, designed [Ingalls Rink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ingalls_Rink), dedicated in 1959,[^cite_note-144] as well as the residential colleges Ezra Stiles and Morse.[^cite_note-145] These latter were modeled after the medieval [Italian hill town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_hill_town) of [San Gimignano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Gimignano)—a prototype chosen for the town's pedestrian-friendly milieu and fortress-like stone towers.[^cite_note-146] These tower forms at Yale act in counterpoint to the college's many Gothic spires and Georgian cupolas.[^cite_note-147]

The athletic field complex is partially in New Haven, and partially in [West Haven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Haven,_Connecticut).[^cite_note-148]



Yale's Old Campus at dusk, April 2013

### Notable nonresidential campus buildings



[Harkness Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harkness_Tower)

Notable nonresidential campus buildings and landmarks include [Battell Chapel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battell_Chapel), [Beinecke Rare Book Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beinecke_Rare_Book_Library), [Harkness Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harkness_Tower), [Humanities Quadrangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanities_Quadrangle), [Ingalls Rink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ingalls_Rink), Kline Biology Tower, [Osborne Memorial Laboratories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osborne_Memorial_Laboratories), [Payne Whitney Gymnasium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Payne_Whitney_Gymnasium), [Peabody Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peabody_Museum_of_Natural_History), Sterling Hall of Medicine, [Sterling Law Buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sterling_Law_Buildings), [Sterling Memorial Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sterling_Memorial_Library), [Woolsey Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woolsey_Hall), [Yale Center for British Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Center_for_British_Art), [Yale University Art Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_University_Art_Gallery), [Yale Art & Architecture Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Art_&_Architecture_Building), and the [Paul Mellon Centre for Studies in British Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Mellon_Centre_for_Studies_in_British_Art) in London.

Yale's secret society buildings (some of which are called "tombs") were built to be private yet unmistakable. A diversity of architectural styles is represented: [Berzelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berzelius_\(secret_society\)), [Donn Barber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donn_Barber) in an austere cube with classical detailing (erected in 1908 or 1910); [Book and Snake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_and_Snake), Louis R. Metcalfe in a [Greek Ionic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Ionic) style (erected in 1901); [Elihu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elihu_\(secret_society\)), architect unknown but built in a [Colonial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_colonial_architecture) style (constructed on an early 17th-century foundation although the building is from the 18th century); [Mace and Chain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mace_and_Chain), in a late colonial, early [Victorian style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_fashion) (built in 1823). (Interior moulding is said to have belonged to [Benedict Arnold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benedict_Arnold)); [Manuscript Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuscript_Society_\(Yale_University\)), [King-lui Wu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King-lui_Wu) with [Dan Kiley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Kiley) responsible for landscaping and [Josef Albers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josef_Albers) for the brickwork intaglio mural. Building constructed in a [mid-century modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-century_modern) style; [Scroll and Key](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scroll_and_Key), [Richard Morris Hunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Morris_Hunt) in a Moorish- or Islamic-inspired [Beaux-Arts style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaux-Arts_architecture) (erected 1869–70); [Skull and Bones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skull_and_Bones), possibly [Alexander Jackson Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Jackson_Davis) or [Henry Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Austin_\(architect\)) in an [Egypto-Doric style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Revival) utilizing [Brownstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brownstone) (in 1856 the first wing was completed, in 1903 the second wing, 1911 the [Neo-Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Gothic) towers in rear garden were completed); [St. Elmo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Elmo_\(secret_society\)), (former tomb) [Kenneth M. Murchison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenneth_MacKenzie_Murchison), 1912, designs inspired by Elizabethan manor. Current location, brick colonial; and [Wolf's Head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf's_Head_\(secret_society\)), [Bertram Grosvenor Goodhue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bertram_Grosvenor_Goodhue), erected 1923–1924, Collegiate Gothic.

### Sustainability

Yale's Office of Sustainability develops and implements sustainability practices at Yale.[^cite_note-Yale_Sustainability_Strategy-149] Yale is committed to reduce its greenhouse gas emissions 10% below 1990 levels by 2020. As part of this commitment, the university allocates renewable energy credits to offset some of the energy used by residential colleges.[^cite_note-Yale_commits_to_long-term_Greenhouse_Gas_Reduction_and_Renewable_Energy_Strategy-150] Eleven campus buildings are candidates for LEED design and certification.[^cite_note-Yale's_Greenhouse_Gas_Reduction_Strategy-151] Yale was listed as a Campus Sustainability Leader on the Sustainable Endowments Institute's College Sustainability Report Card 2008, and received a "B+" grade overall.[^cite_note-Sustainable_Endowments_Institute_Report_Card-152] Yale is a member of the Ivy Plus Sustainability Consortium, through which it has committed to best-practice sharing and the ongoing exchange of campus sustainability solutions along with other member institutions.[^cite_note-Leadership_Through_Partnership-153]

### Relationship with New Haven

Yale is the largest taxpayer and employer in the City of [New Haven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Haven),[^cite_note-154] and has often buoyed the city's economy and communities. Yale, however, has consistently opposed paying a tax on its academic property.[^cite_note-155] Yale's [Art Galleries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_University_Art_Gallery), along with many other university resources, are free and openly accessible. Yale also funds the [New Haven Promise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Haven,_Connecticut#New_Haven_Promise) program, paying full tuition for eligible students from New Haven public schools.[^cite_note-156]

#### Town–gown relations

Yale has a complicated relationship with its home city; for example, thousands of students volunteer every year in myriad community organizations, but city officials, who decry Yale's exemption from local property taxes, have long pressed the university to do more to help. Under President Levin, Yale has financially supported many of New Haven's efforts to reinvigorate the city. Evidence suggests that the [town and gown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Town_and_gown) relationships are mutually beneficial. Still, the economic power of the university increased dramatically with its financial success amid a decline in the local economy.[^cite_note-157]

#### Campus safety

The first campus police force was founded at Yale in 1894, when the university contracted city police officers to exclusively cover the campus.[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159] Later hired by the university, the officers were originally brought in to quell unrest between students and city residents and curb destructive student behavior.[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161]

In the 1970s and 1980s, [poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_locations_by_per_capita_income) and [violent crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violent_crime) rose in New Haven, dampening Yale's student and faculty recruiting efforts.[^cite_note-162] Between 1990 and 2006, New Haven's crime rate fell by half, helped by a [community policing strategy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_policing) by the [New Haven Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Haven_Police_Department) and Yale's campus became the safest among peer schools.[^cite_note-163]

In 2004, the national non-profit watchdog group Security on Campus filed a complaint with the [U.S. Department of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Department_of_Education), accusing Yale of under-reporting rape and sexual assaults.[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165]

## Academics

### Admissions

Undergraduate admission to Yale College is considered "most selective" by *[U.S. News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._News_&_World_Report)*.[^cite_note-U.S._News_&_World_Report-166][^cite_note-167] In 2022, Yale accepted 2,234 students to the Class of 2026 out of 50,015 applicants, for an acceptance rate of 4.46%.[^cite_note-168] In 2026, the admission rate for the class of 2030 fell to 4.2% from 4.59% the prior year, with 2,328 students accepted out of a pool of 54,919 applicants (the second largest in Yale's history following the class of 2028).[^cite_note-169] 98% of students graduate within six years.[^cite_note-2013YCBN-170] Yale's most selective graduate schools are: the [Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Law_School) (4%),[^cite_note-171] [School of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Medicine) (5%),[^cite_note-172] [Graduate School of Arts and Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Graduate_School_of_Arts_and_Sciences) (5.7% - only for PhD admissions),[^cite_note-173] [School of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Art) (6%),[^cite_note-174] [School of Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Music) (10%),[^cite_note-175] [School of Architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Architecture) (15%),[^cite_note-176] and [Divinity School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Divinity_School) (15%).[^cite_note-177] Yale Divinity School is notable for being the most selective institution for the study of religion in the world.[^cite_note-178]

Through its program of need-based financial aid, Yale commits to meet the full demonstrated financial need of all applicants, and the university is [need-blind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Need-blind) for both domestic and international applicants.[^cite_note-179] Most financial aid is in the form of grants and scholarships that do not need to be paid back to the university, and the average need-based aid grant for the Class of 2017 was \$46,395.[^cite_note-2013CDS-180] 15% of Yale College students are expected to have no parental contribution, and about 50% receive some form of financial aid.[^cite_note-2013YCBN-170][^cite_note-181][^cite_note-FinAidPolicy-182] About 16% of the Class of 2013 had some form of student loan debt at graduation, with an average debt of \$13,000 among borrowers.[^cite_note-2013YCBN-170] For 2019, Yale ranked second in enrollment of recipients of the [National Merit \$2,500 Scholarship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Merit_Scholarship_Program) (140 scholars).[^cite_note-183]

Half of all Yale undergraduates are women, more than 39% are [ethnic minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_minority) U.S. citizens (19% are underrepresented minorities), and 10.5% are [international students](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_student).[^cite_note-2013CDS-180] 55% attended public schools and 45% attended private, religious, or international schools, and 97% of students were in the top 10% of their high school class.[^cite_note-2013YCBN-170] Every year, Yale College also admits a small group of [non-traditional students](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-traditional_student) through the Eli Whitney Students Program.[^cite_note-184]

### Collections



Yale University's [Sterling Memorial Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sterling_Memorial_Library), as seen from [Maya Lin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_Lin)'s sculpture, *Women's Table*. The sculpture records the number of women enrolled at Yale over its history; female undergraduates were not admitted until 1969.

[Yale University Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_University_Library), which holds over 15 million volumes, is the second-largest university collection in the United States after [Harvard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_University).[^cite_note-2013LibraryReport-13][^cite_note-185] The main library, [Sterling Memorial Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sterling_Memorial_Library), contains about 4 million volumes, and other holdings are dispersed at subject and location libraries. For the 2022 fiscal year, the university reported 9,144,904 physical library holdings and 2,784,465 holdings in their digital/electronic collection.[^cite_note-186]

Rare books are found in several Yale collections. The [Beinecke Rare Book Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beinecke_Rare_Book_Library) has a large collection of rare books and manuscripts. The [Harvey Cushing/John Hay Whitney Medical Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvey_Cushing/John_Hay_Whitney_Medical_Library) includes important historical medical texts, including an impressive collection of rare books, as well as historical medical instruments. The [Lewis Walpole Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_Walpole_Library) contains the largest collection of 18th‑century British literary works. The [Elizabethan Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabethan_Club), technically a private organization, makes its Elizabethan folios and first editions available to qualified researchers through Yale.



*[The Night Café](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Night_Caf%C3%A9)*, Vincent van Gogh, 1888, [Yale Art Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_University_Art_Gallery)

Yale's museum collections are also of international stature. The [Yale University Art Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_University_Art_Gallery), the country's first university-affiliated art museum, contains more than 200,000 works, including Old Masters and important collections of modern art, in the Swartwout and Kahn buildings. The latter, [Louis Kahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Kahn)'s first large-scale American work (1953), was renovated and reopened in December 2006. The [Yale Center for British Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Center_for_British_Art), the largest collection of British art outside of the UK, grew from a gift of [Paul Mellon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Mellon) and is housed in another Kahn-designed building.

The [Peabody Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peabody_Museum_of_Natural_History) in New Haven is used by school children and contains research collections in anthropology, archaeology, and the natural environment.

The [Yale University Collection of Musical Instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_University_Collection_of_Musical_Instruments), affiliated with the Yale School of Music, is perhaps the least-known of Yale's collections because its hours of opening are restricted.

The museums once housed the artifacts brought to the United States from [Peru](170691.md) by Yale history professor [Hiram Bingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiram_Bingham_III) in his Yale-financed expedition to [Machu Picchu](80019.md) in 1912—when the removal of such artifacts was legal. The artifacts were restored to Peru in 2012.[^cite_note-187]

### Rankings and reputation

The *[U.S. News & World Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._News_&_World_Report)* ranked Yale fourth among U.S. national universities for 2025 (tied with [Stanford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanford_University)).[^cite_note-196] [Niche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niche_\(company\)) ranks Yale as the 2nd best university in [America](3434750.md).[^cite_note-197]Yale University is [accredited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education_accreditation_in_the_United_States) by the [New England Commission of Higher Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England_Commission_of_Higher_Education).[^cite_note-198]

Internationally, Yale was ranked 11th in the 2025 [Academic Ranking of World Universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_Ranking_of_World_Universities).[^cite_note-199]

In 2026, it was ranked 2nd among the world’s top universities by [*Time*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\)) magazine and [Statista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statista).[^cite_note-200]

### Faculty, research, and intellectual traditions

Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence). *(March 2021)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

Yale is a member of the [Association of American Universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_American_Universities) (AAU) and is [classified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Classification_of_Institutions_of_Higher_Education) among "R1: Doctoral Universities—Very high research activity".[^cite_note-201] The [National Science Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Science_Foundation) ranked Yale 16th among American universities for research and development expenditures in 2024 with \$1.52 billion.[^cite_note-202]

Yale's current faculty include 67 members of the [National Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Sciences),[^cite_note-National_Academy_of_Sciences-203] 55 members of the [National Academy of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Medicine),[^cite_note-National_Academy_of_Medicine-204] 8 members of the [National Academy of Engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Engineering),[^cite_note-NAE_Website-205] and 187 members of the [American Academy of Arts and Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Academy_of_Arts_and_Sciences).[^cite_note-American_Academy_of_Arts_and_Sciences-206] The college is, after normalization for institution size, the tenth-largest baccalaureate source of [doctoral degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctorate) recipients in the United States, and the largest such source within the Ivy League.[^cite_note-Center_College-207] It also is a top 10 (ranked seventh) baccalaureate source (after normalization for the number of graduates) of some of the most notable scientists ([Nobel](21201.md), [Fields](10859.md), [Turing](31093.md) prizes, or membership in [National Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Sciences), [National Academy of Engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Engineering), or [National Academy of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Medicine)).[^cite_note-Wai-2015-208]

Yale's English and Comparative Literature departments were part of the [New Criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Criticism) movement. Of the New Critics, [Robert Penn Warren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Penn_Warren), [W.K. Wimsatt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W.K._Wimsatt), and [Cleanth Brooks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleanth_Brooks) were all Yale faculty. Later, the Yale Comparative literature department became a center of American [deconstruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deconstruction). [Jacques Derrida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Derrida), the father of deconstruction, taught at the department of comparative literature from the late 1970s to mid-1980s. Several other Yale faculty members were also associated with deconstruction, forming the so-called "[Yale school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_school)".These included [Paul de Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_de_Man) who taught in the departments of comparative literature and French, [J. Hillis Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Hillis_Miller), [Geoffrey Hartman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Hartman) (both taught in the departments of English and comparative literature), and [Harold Bloom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Bloom) (English), whose theoretical position was always somewhat specific, and who ultimately took a very different path from the rest of this group. Yale's history department has also originated important intellectual trends. Historians [C. Vann Woodward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._Vann_Woodward) and [David Brion Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Brion_Davis) are credited with beginning in the 1960s and 1970s an important stream of [southern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_South) historians; likewise, [David Montgomery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Montgomery_\(historian\)), a labor historian, advised many of the current generation of labor historians in the country. Yale's Music School and department fostered the growth of Music Theory in the latter half of the 20th century. The *Journal of Music Theory* was founded there in 1957; [Allen Forte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allen_Forte) and [David Lewin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Lewin) were influential teachers and scholars.

Since the late 1960s, Yale produces social sciences and policy research through its Yale Institution for Social and Policy Studies (ISPS).

In addition to eminent faculty members, Yale research relies heavily on the presence of roughly 1200 [Postdocs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postdoctoral_researcher) from various national and international origin working in the multiple laboratories in the sciences, social sciences, humanities, and professional schools of the university. The university progressively recognized this working force with the recent creation of the Office for Postdoctoral Affairs and the [Yale Postdoctoral Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Postdoctoral_Association).

## Campus life
