Yale is a research university, with the majority of its students in the [graduate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graduate_school) and [professional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professional_school) schools. [Undergraduates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undergraduate_education), or [Yale College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_College) students, come from a variety of ethnic, national, socioeconomic, and personal backgrounds. Of the 2010–2011 freshman class, 10% are non‑U.S. citizens, while 54% went to public high schools.[^cite_note-212] The median family income of Yale students is \$192,600, with 57% of students coming from the top 10% highest-earning families and 16% from the bottom 60%.[^cite_note-NYT_mobility_index-213]

### Residential colleges

Main article: [Residential colleges of Yale University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Residential_colleges_of_Yale_University)

Yale's [residential college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Residential_college) system was established in 1933 by [Edward S. Harkness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_S._Harkness), who admired the social intimacy of Oxford and Cambridge and donated significant funds to found similar [colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collegiate_university) at Yale and Harvard. Though Yale's colleges resemble their English precursors organizationally and architecturally, they are [dependent entities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collegiate_university) of Yale College and have limited autonomy. The colleges are led by a head and an academic dean, who reside in the college, and university faculty and affiliates constitute each college's fellowship. Colleges offer their own seminars, social events, and speaking engagements known as "Master's teas", but do not contain programs of study or academic departments. All other undergraduate courses are taught by the Faculty of Arts and Sciences and are open to members of any college.

All undergraduates are members of a college, to which they are assigned before their freshman year, and 85 percent live in the college quadrangle or a college-affiliated dormitory.[^cite_note-214] While the majority of upperclassman live in the colleges, most on-campus freshmen live on the [Old Campus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Campus), the university's oldest precinct.

While Harkness's original colleges were [Georgian Revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_architecture) or [Collegiate Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collegiate_Gothic) in style, two colleges constructed in the 1960s, [Morse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morse_College) and [Ezra Stiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ezra_Stiles_College) Colleges, have modernist designs. All twelve college quadrangles are organized around a courtyard, and each has a dining hall, courtyard, library, common room, and a range of student facilities. The twelve colleges are named for important alumni or significant places in university history. In 2017, the university opened two new colleges near [Science Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_Hill_\(Yale_University\)).[^cite_note-215] \

#### Calhoun College

Since the 1960s, [John C. Calhoun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_C._Calhoun)'s [white supremacist](33178.md) beliefs and pro-slavery leadership[^cite_note-calhoun_1837-216][^cite_note-student-petition-2015-217][^cite_note-theatlantic_2015_rename_Calhoun-218][^cite_note-Freshman_address_aug_2015-219] had prompted calls to rename the college or remove its tributes to Calhoun. The racially motivated [church shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charleston_church_shooting) in [Charleston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charleston,_South_Carolina), South Carolina, led to renewed calls in the summer of 2015 for [Calhoun College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calhoun_College), one of 12 residential colleges at the time, to be renamed. In July 2015 students signed a petition calling for the name change.[^cite_note-student-petition-2015-217] They argued in the petition that—while Calhoun was respected in the 19th century as an "extraordinary American statesman"—he was "one of the most prolific defenders of slavery and white supremacy" in the history of the United States.[^cite_note-student-petition-2015-217][^cite_note-theatlantic_2015_rename_Calhoun-218] In August 2015, Yale President Peter Salovey addressed the freshman class of 2019 in which he responded to the racial tensions but explained why the college would not be renamed.[^cite_note-Freshman_address_aug_2015-219] He described Calhoun as "a notable political theorist, a vice president to two different U.S. presidents, a secretary of war and of state, and a congressman and senator representing South Carolina".[^cite_note-Freshman_address_aug_2015-219] He acknowledged that Calhoun also "believed that the highest forms of civilization depend on involuntary servitude. Not only that, but he also believed that the races he thought to be inferior, black people in particular, ought to be subjected to it for the sake of their own best interests."[^cite_note-calhoun_1837-216] Student activism about this issue increased in the fall of 2015, and included further protests sparked by controversy surrounding an administrator's comments on the potential positive and negative implications of students who wear [Halloween costumes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halloween_costumes) that are [culturally sensitive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culturally_sensitive).[^cite_note-220] Campus-wide discussions expanded to include critical discussion of the experiences of women of color on campus, and the realities of racism in undergraduate life.[^cite_note-221] The protests were sensationalized by the media and led to the labelling of some students as being members of [Generation Snowflake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generation_Snowflake).[^cite_note-222]

In April 2016, Salovey announced that "despite decades of vigorous alumni and student protests", Calhoun's name would remain on the Yale residential college[^cite_note-NYT_April_2016_right_wrong-223] explaining that it is preferable for Yale students to live in Calhoun's "shadow" so they will be "better prepared to rise to the challenges of the present and the future." He claimed that if they removed Calhoun's name, it would "obscure" his "legacy of slavery rather than addressing it".[^cite_note-NYT_April_2016_right_wrong-223] "Yale is part of that history" and "We cannot erase American history, but we can confront it, teach it and learn from it." One change that will be issued is the title of "master" for faculty members who serve as residential college leaders will be renamed to "head of college" due to its connotation of slavery.[^cite_note-foxnews_2015-224]

In July 2016, an African-American dishwasher named Corey Menafee destroyed a stained-glass depiction of slaves in the Calhoun College dining hall. He was initially fired and prosecuted; after protests from alumni and local activists, the university reinstated him and asked the prosecutor to drop the charges. Soon after, Salovey appointed a committee to determine factors relevant in renaming a building.[^cite_note-Bass-225] In February 2017, Salovey announced that Calhoun College would be renamed for groundbreaking computer scientist [Grace Hopper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grace_Hopper).[^cite_note-226] This renaming decision received a range of responses from Yale students and alumni.[^cite_note-227][^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229] In his 2019 book *Assault on American Excellence*, former [Dean of Yale Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dean_of_Yale_Law_School) [Anthony T. Kronman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_T._Kronman) criticized the title and name changes and the lack of support from Salovey for [the Christakises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_Christakis#Yale_Halloween_controversy), who were targeted by the student activists. Other members of the university community disagreed with Kronman's positions.[^cite_note-230]

### Student organizations

In 2024, Yale had 526 registered undergraduate student organizations, plus hundreds of others for graduate students.[^cite_note-231]

Dwight Hall, an independent, non-profit community service organization, oversees more than 2,000 Yale undergraduates working on more than 70 community service initiatives in New Haven. The Yale College Council runs several agencies that oversee campus-wide activities and student services. The [Yale Dramatic Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Dramatic_Association) and Bulldog Productions cater to the theater and film communities, respectively. In addition, the Yale Drama Coalition[^cite_note-232] serves to coordinate between and provide resources for the various Sudler Fund sponsored theater productions which run each weekend. WYBC Yale Radio[^cite_note-233] is the campus's radio station, owned and operated by students. While students used to broadcast on AM and FM frequencies, they now have an Internet-only stream.

The [Yale College Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_College_Council) (YCC) serves as the campus's undergraduate student government. All registered student organizations are regulated and funded by a subsidiary organization of the YCC, known as the Undergraduate Organizations Funding Committee (UOFC).[^cite_note-UOFC-234] The Graduate and Professional Student Senate (GPSS) serves as Yale's graduate and professional student government.

[The Yale Political Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Yale_Political_Union) (YPU) is a debate society founded in 1934 to host student discussions on a wide variety of topics. It is advised by alumni political leaders such as [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry) and [George Pataki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Pataki).

The [Yale International Relations Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_International_Relations_Association) (YIRA) functions as the umbrella organization for the university's top-ranked Model UN team. YIRA also has a Europe-based offshoot, [Yale Model Government Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Model_Government_Europe), other Model UN conferences such as YMUN, YMUN Korea, YMUN Taiwan and Yale Model African Union (YMAU), the *[Yale Review of International Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Review_of_International_Studies)* (*YRIS*), and educational programs such as the Yale International Relations Leadership Institute and Hemispheres.

The campus includes several [fraternities and sororities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraternities_and_sororities). The campus features at least 18 [a cappella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_cappella) groups, the most famous of which is [The Whiffenpoofs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Whiffenpoofs), which from its founding in 1909 until 2018 was made up solely of senior men.

The [Elizabethan Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabethan_Club), a social club, has a membership of undergraduates, graduates, faculty and staff with literary or artistic interests. Membership is by invitation. Members and their guests may enter the "Lizzie's" premises for conversation and tea. The club owns first editions of a Shakespeare Folio, several Shakespeare Quartos, and a first edition of Milton's *[Paradise Lost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paradise_Lost)*, among other important literary texts.

#### Secret societies

Main article: [Yale secret societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_secret_societies)

Yale's [secret societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secret_society) [include](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secret_Societies_at_Yale_University) [Skull and Bones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skull_and_Bones), [Scroll and Key](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scroll_and_Key), [Wolf's Head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf's_Head_\(secret_society\)), [Book and Snake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_and_Snake), [Elihu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elihu_\(secret_society\)), [Berzelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berzelius_\(secret_society\)), [St. Elmo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Elmo_\(secret_society\)), [Manuscript](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuscript_Society_\(Yale_University\)), [Brothers in Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brothers_in_Unity), [Linonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linonia), [St. Anthony Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Anthony_Hall), [Shabtai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabtai_\(society\)), [Myth and Sword](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myth_and_Sword), Daughters of Sovereign Government (DSG), Sphinx, [Mace and Chain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mace_and_Chain), ISO, [Spade and Grave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spade_and_Grave), [Sage and Chalice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sage_and_Chalice), and [Ink and Needle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ink_and_Needle?action=edit\&redlink=1) among others. The two oldest existing honor societies are the [Aurelian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurelian_Honor_Society) (1910) and the [Torch Honor Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torch_Honor_Society) (1916).[^cite_note-235]

These are akin to [Harvard finals clubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_College_social_clubs), [Princeton eating clubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eating_clubs_at_Princeton_University), and [senior societies at University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senior_societies_at_University_of_Pennsylvania).

### Student publications

The university hosts a variety of student journals, magazines, and newspapers. The *[Yale Literary Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Literary_Magazine)*, founded in February 1836, is the oldest student literary magazine in the United States.[^cite_note-236]

Established in 1872, *[The Yale Record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Yale_Record)* is the world's oldest college [humor magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humor_magazine). The *Yale Journal of Medicine & Law* is a biannual magazine that explores the intersection of [law and medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_law).

Newspapers include the *[Yale Daily News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Daily_News)*, which was first published in 1878, and the weekly *[Yale Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Herald)*, which was first published in 1986.

*The Yale Globalist* is an undergraduate magazine focused on [international affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_affairs). The first of four issues published annually is about a specific country, which has been visited by a small group of student reporters. The other three issues are each around topics chosen democratically by the group of student editors.[^cite_note-237]

### Traditions

See also: [Bladderball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bladderball)

Yale seniors at graduation smash clay pipes underfoot to symbolize passage from their "[bright college years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bright_College_Years)", though in recent history the pipes have been replaced with "bubble pipes".[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239] ("Bright College Years", the university's alma mater, was penned in 1881 by [Henry Durand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Strong_Durand), Class of 1881, to the tune of *[Die Wacht am Rhein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Die_Wacht_am_Rhein)*.) Yale's student tour guides tell visitors that students consider it good luck to rub the toe of the statue of [Theodore Dwight Woolsey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Dwight_Woolsey) on Old Campus; however, actual students rarely do so.[^cite_note-240] In the second half of the 20th century [Bladderball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bladderball), a campus-wide game played with a large inflatable ball, became a popular tradition but was banned by administration due to safety concerns. In spite of administration opposition, students revived the game in 2009, 2011, and 2014.[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243]

### Athletics

Main article: [Yale Bulldogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Bulldogs)



Yale Athletics Logo



The [Yale Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Bowl), the college football stadium

Yale supports 35 varsity athletic teams that compete in the [Ivy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivy_League) Conference, the [Eastern College Athletic Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_College_Athletic_Conference), and the [New England Intercollegiate Sailing Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England_Intercollegiate_Sailing_Association). Yale athletic teams compete intercollegiately at the [NCAA](60706.md) Division I level. Like other members of the Ivy League, Yale does not offer athletic scholarships.

Yale has numerous athletic facilities, including the [Yale Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Bowl) (the nation's first natural "bowl" stadium, and prototype for such stadiums as the [Los Angeles Memorial Coliseum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Memorial_Coliseum) and the [Rose Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_Bowl_\(stadium\))), located at The [Walter Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Camp) Field athletic complex, and the [Payne Whitney Gymnasium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Payne_Whitney_Gymnasium), the second-largest indoor athletic complex in the world.[^cite_note-244]

In 1970, the NCAA banned Yale from participating in all NCAA sports for two years, in reaction to Yale—against the wishes of the NCAA—playing its Jewish center [Jack Langer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Langer) in college games after Langer had played for Team United States at the [1969 Maccabiah Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969_Maccabiah_Games) in Israel with the approval of Yale President [Kingman Brewster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingman_Brewster).[^cite_note-auto11a-245][^cite_note-autoa-246][^cite_note-auto3a-247][^cite_note-auto12a-248] The decision impacted 300 Yale students, every Yale student on its sports teams, over the next two years.[^cite_note-auto15a-249]

In 2016, the men's basketball team won the Ivy League Championship title for the first time in 54 years, earning a spot in the [NCAA Division I men's basketball tournament](507399.md). In the first round of the tournament, the Bulldogs beat the [Baylor Bears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baylor_Bears) 79–75 in the school's first-ever tournament win.[^cite_note-250]



The [Walter Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Camp) Gate at the Yale Athletic Complex

In May 2018, the men's lacrosse team defeated the [Duke Blue Devils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Blue_Devils) to claim their first-ever [NCAA Division I Men's Lacrosse Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I_Men's_Lacrosse_Championship),[^cite_note-251] and were the first Ivy League school to win the title since the [Princeton Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_Tigers) in 2001.[^cite_note-252]

Yale crew is the oldest collegiate athletic team in America, and won [Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympics) [Gold Medal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_Medal) for men's eights in 1924 and 1956. Over 170 Yale alumni have competed in the Olympic games and have won over 110 medals.[^cite_note-news.yale.edu-19]

The [Yale Corinthian Yacht Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Corinthian_Yacht_Club), founded in 1881, is the oldest collegiate [sailing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sailing_\(sport\)) club in the world. October 21, 2000, marked the dedication of Yale's fourth new boathouse in 157 years of collegiate rowing. The [Gilder Boathouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilder_Boathouse) is named to honor former Olympic rower Virginia Gilder '79 and her father [Richard Gilder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Gilder) '54, who gave \$4 million towards the \$7.5 million project. Yale also maintains the [Gales Ferry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gales_Ferry) site where the heavyweight men's team trains for the [Yale-Harvard Boat Race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale-Harvard_Boat_Race).

In 1896, Yale and [Johns Hopkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johns_Hopkins_University) played the first known [ice hockey](14790.md) game in the United States. Since 2006, the school's ice hockey clubs have played a commemorative game.[^cite_note-253]

Yale students claim to have invented [Frisbee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frisbee), by tossing empty [Frisbie Pie Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frisbie_Pie_Company) tins.[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255]

Yale athletics are supported by the [Yale Precision Marching Band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Precision_Marching_Band). "Precision" is used here ironically; the band is a scatter-style band that runs wildly between formations rather than actually marching.[^cite_note-256] The band attends every home football game and many away, as well as most hockey and basketball games throughout the winter.

Yale intramural sports are also a significant aspect of student life. Students compete for their respective residential colleges, fostering a friendly rivalry. The year is divided into fall, winter, and spring seasons, each of which includes about 10 different sports. About half the sports are coeducational. At the end of the year, the residential college with the most points (not all sports count equally) wins the Tyng Cup.

#### Song

Notable among the songs commonly played and sung at events such as [commencement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graduation), [convocation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convocation), alumni gatherings, and athletic games is the alma mater, "[Bright College Years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bright_College_Years)". Despite its popularity, "[Boola Boola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boola_Boola)" is not the official [fight song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fight_song), albeit being the origin of the university's unofficial motto. The official Yale fight song, "Bulldog" was written by [Cole Porter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cole_Porter) during his undergraduate days and is sung after touchdowns during a football game.[^cite_note-257] Additionally, two other songs, "Down the Field" by C.W. O'Conner, and "Bingo Eli Yale", also by Cole Porter, are still sung at football games. According to *College Fight Songs: An Annotated Anthology* published in 1998, "Down the Field" ranks as the fourth-greatest fight song of all time.[^cite_note-258]

#### Mascot

The school [mascot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mascot) is [Handsome Dan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handsome_Dan), the Yale [bulldog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulldog), and the Yale fight song contains the refrain, "Bulldog, bulldog, bow wow wow." The school color, since 1894, is [Yale Blue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Blue).[^cite_note-259] Yale's Handsome Dan is believed to be the first college mascot in America, having been established in 1889.[^cite_note-YaleBulldogs-260]

### Mental health

Yale has faced significant criticism for its handling of [student mental health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_health_in_education) on campus.[^cite_note-Wan2022-261][^cite_note-Wanfollowup-262][^cite_note-delValle2022-263][^cite_note-Giambrone2015-264] Suicidal and depressed students say that Yale forced them to medically withdraw rather than provide them with academic accommodations under the [Americans with Disabilities Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americans_with_Disabilities_Act), and in 2018 the [Ruderman Family Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruderman_Family_Foundation) ranked Yale as having the worst mental health policies in the Ivy League.[^cite_note-Bialek2021-265][^cite_note-Heyman2018-266][^cite_note-delValle2022-263]

> Dear Yale, I loved being here. I only wish I could've had some time. I needed time to work things out and to wait for new medication to kick in, but I couldn't do it in school, and I couldn't bear the thought of having to leave for a full year, or of leaving and never being readmitted. Love, Luchang.

— Luchang Wang, posted on Facebook in 2015 shortly before her death

[^cite_note-Wan2022-261]

[^cite_note-Giambrone2015-264]

[^cite_note-Siegel2015-267]

[^cite_note-268]

[^cite_note-Miller2016-269]

Students at Yale say that the university's policies force them to hide their depression and avoid seeking help, for fear of being forced to leave.[^cite_note-Wan2022-261][^cite_note-Bialek2021-265][^cite_note-Giambrone2015-264] One prominent case was the suicide of Luchang Wang in 2015, who died by suicide after making a Facebook post saying that she needed time to deal with her mental health issues, but could not deal with being forced to medically withdraw for an entire year with an uncertain chance of being readmitted.[^cite_note-Siegel2015-267][^cite_note-Giambrone2015-264][^cite_note-Miller2016-269] Wang had previously withdrawn from school due to mental health issues, and was afraid of being forced to withdraw again, as a second readmission attempt would be considerably more difficult for her.[^cite_note-Siegel2015-267][^cite_note-Giambrone2015-264] A friend of Wang said that she routinely lied to her university therapist to avoid being kicked out,[^cite_note-Siegel2015-267] and another student said that many at Yale lie to their counselors as "there's no clear standard established that says exactly what students will get involuntarily hospitalized or withdrawn for."[^cite_note-Giambrone2015-264] In response, the university convened a commission to evaluate their readmission policies after a mental health withdrawal, renaming the process to "reinstatement", eliminating the \$50 reapplication fee, and giving students 5–6 more days to make their decision on a mental health withdrawal.[^cite_note-Wan2022-261][^cite_note-270]

For students that do seek help, waitlists for therapy can be months long, with individual counselling sessions only 30 minutes in length.[^cite_note-Wan2022-261] In 2022, after a *Washington Post* article about their medical withdrawal policies, the school increased the number of mental health clinicians on campus from 51 to 60 as well as promised further changes.[^cite_note-Wanfollowup-262] In 2023, after a lawsuit was filed against the school for what the plaintiffs described as discrimination, the university changed the name of a "medical withdrawal" to a "medical leave of absence" saying that the "leave of absence" terminology would allow students to remain on Yale's insurance while away from the school.[^cite_note-Cook2023-271][^cite_note-Hartocollis-272] The new policy also allowed for students on a leave of absence to participate in extracurricular clubs and visit campus,[^cite_note-Cook2023-271][^cite_note-Hartocollis-272] something a student on medical withdrawal was banned from doing.[^cite_note-Wan2022-261] A representative of Yale also said that the criticism of their policies "misrepresents our efforts and unwavering commitment to supporting our students, whose well-being and success are our primary focus" and that "the mental health of our students is a very, very high priority."[^cite_note-Wanfollowup-262]

After the death of undergraduate student Rachael Shaw Rosenbaum by suicide, an organization called Elis for Rachael was formed, advocating for mental health-related reforms. The group has sued Yale, demanding changes.[^cite_note-273]

## Notable people

### Benefactors

Yale has had many financial supporters, but some stand out by the magnitude or timeliness of their contributions. Among those who have made large donations commemorated at the university are: [Elihu Yale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elihu_Yale), [Jeremiah Dummer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremiah_Dummer), the [Vanderbilt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanderbilt_family) family, the [Harkness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harkness_Tower) family ([Edward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_S._Harkness), [Anna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anna_M._Harkness), and [William](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Harkness)), the [Beinecke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beinecke_Rare_Book_and_Manuscript_Library) family (Edwin, [Frederick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_W._Beinecke), and Walter), [John William Sterling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_William_Sterling), [Payne Whitney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Payne_Whitney), [Joseph Earl Sheffield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Earl_Sheffield), [Paul Mellon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Mellon), [Charles B. G. Murphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_B._G._Murphy), [Joseph Tsai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Tsai), [William K. Lanman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_K._Lanman), and [Stephen Schwarzman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_A._Schwarzman). The Yale Class of 1954, led by [Richard Gilder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Gilder), donated \$70 million in commemoration of their 50th reunion.[^cite_note-274] [Charles B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_B._Johnson), a 1954 graduate of Yale College, pledged a \$250 million gift in 2013 to support the construction of two new residential colleges.[^cite_note-275] The colleges have been named respectively in honor of [Pauli Murray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauli_Murray) and [Benjamin Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin). A \$100 million contribution[^cite_note-276] by [Stephen Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Adams_\(businessman\)) enabled the [Yale School of Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Music) to become tuition-free and the Adams Center for Musical Arts to be built, while a \$150 million contribution[^cite_note-277] by [David Geffen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Geffen) enabled the Yale School of Drama (renamed the [David Geffen School of Drama at Yale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_School_of_Drama)) to become tuition-free as well.

Yale has many distinguished alumni, in both the public and private sectors. According to 2020 data, around 71% of undergraduates join the workforce, while 17% attend graduate or professional schools.[^cite_note-278] Yale graduates have been recipients of 263 [Rhodes Scholarships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhodes_Scholarship),[^cite_note-279] 123 [Marshall Scholarships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Scholarship),[^cite_note-280] 67 [Truman Scholarships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_S._Truman_Scholarship),[^cite_note-281] 21 [Churchill Scholarships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Churchill_Scholarship),[^cite_note-282] and 9 [Mitchell Scholarships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitchell_Scholarship).[^cite_note-283] The university is the 2nd largest producer of [Fulbright Scholars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fulbright_Program), with 1,244 in its history[^cite_note-284] and 89 [MacArthur Fellows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacArthur_Fellows_Program).[^cite_note-285] The U.S. Department of State Bureau of Educational and Cultural Affairs ranked Yale fifth among research institutions producing the most 2020–2021 Fulbright Scholars.[^cite_note-286] 31 [living billionaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_by_number_of_billionaire_alumni) are alumni.[^cite_note-Elkins-2018-287]

One of the most popular undergraduate majors is political science, with many alumni going on to serve in government and politics.[^cite_note-288] Former presidents who attended for undergrad include [William Howard Taft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Howard_Taft), [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush), and [George W. Bush](3414021.md), while former presidents [Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford) and [Bill Clinton](3356.md) attended Yale Law School.[^cite_note-289] Among vice presidents, influential [antebellum era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antebellum_era) politician [John C. Calhoun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_C._Calhoun) graduated from Yale,[^cite_note-290] while [JD Vance](53396477.md) graduated from Yale Law School.[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292] Former world leaders include Italian prime minister [Mario Monti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Monti),[^cite_note-293] Turkish prime minister [Tansu Çiller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tansu_%C3%87iller),[^cite_note-294] South Korean prime minister [Lee Hong-koo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Hong-koo),[^cite_note-295] Mexican president [Ernesto Zedillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernesto_Zedillo),[^cite_note-296] German president [Karl Carstens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Carstens),[^cite_note-297] Philippine president [José Paciano Laurel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Paciano_Laurel),[^cite_note-298] Latvian president [Valdis Zatlers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valdis_Zatlers),[^cite_note-299] Taiwanese premier [Jiang Yi-huah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiang_Yi-huah),[^cite_note-300] and Malawian president [Peter Mutharika](29471682.md),[^cite_note-301] among others. Prominent royals who graduated are [Crown Princess Victoria of Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Princess_Victoria_of_Sweden)[^cite_note-302] and [Olympia Bonaparte, Princess Napoléon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympia_von_und_zu_Arco-Zinneberg).[^cite_note-303]

Alumni have had considerable presence in U.S. government in all three branches. On the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md), 19 justices have been alumni, including current [Associate Justices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Associate_Justice_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States) [Sonia Sotomayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonia_Sotomayor),[^cite_note-304] [Samuel Alito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Alito),[^cite_note-Liptak-2014-305] [Clarence Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Thomas),[^cite_note-Liptak-2014-305] and [Brett Kavanaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brett_Kavanaugh).[^cite_note-306] Alumni have been [U.S. Senators](24909346.md), including current senators [Michael Bennet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Bennet),[^cite_note-307] [Richard Blumenthal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Blumenthal),[^cite_note-308] [Cory Booker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cory_Booker),[^cite_note-309] [Sherrod Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sherrod_Brown),[^cite_note-310] [Chris Coons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Coons),[^cite_note-311] [Amy Klobuchar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Klobuchar),[^cite_note-312] [Sheldon Whitehouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheldon_Whitehouse).[^cite_note-313] Current and former cabinet members include Secretaries of State [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry),[^cite_note-314] [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton),[^cite_note-315] [Cyrus Vance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_Vance),[^cite_note-316] and [Dean Acheson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dean_Acheson);[^cite_note-317] U.S. Secretaries of the Treasury [Oliver Wolcott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Wolcott),[^cite_note-318] [Robert Rubin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Rubin),[^cite_note-319] [Nicholas F. Brady](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_F._Brady),[^cite_note-320] [Steven Mnuchin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Mnuchin),[^cite_note-321] [Janet Yellen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janet_Yellen),[^cite_note-322] and [Scott Bessent](38532044.md);[^cite_note-323] U.S. Attorneys General [Nicholas Katzenbach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_Katzenbach),[^cite_note-324] [Edwin Meese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwin_Meese), [John Ashcroft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Ashcroft),[^cite_note-325] and [Edward H. Levi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_H._Levi);[^cite_note-326] and many others. Peace Corps founder and American diplomat [Sargent Shriver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sargent_Shriver)[^cite_note-327] and public official and urban planner [Robert Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Moses)[^cite_note-328] are Yale alumni.

Yale has produced numerous award-winning authors and influential writers,[^cite_note-329] like [Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md) laureate [Sinclair Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinclair_Lewis)[^cite_note-330] and [Pulitzer Prize](24230.md) winners [Stephen Vincent Benét](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Vincent_Ben%C3%A9t),[^cite_note-331] [Thornton Wilder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thornton_Wilder),[^cite_note-332] [Doug Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doug_Wright),[^cite_note-333] and [David McCullough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_McCullough).[^cite_note-334] [Academy Award](324.md) winning actors, actresses, and directors include [Jodie Foster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jodie_Foster),[^cite_note-335] [Paul Newman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Newman),[^cite_note-336] [Meryl Streep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meryl_Streep),[^cite_note-337] [Elia Kazan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elia_Kazan),[^cite_note-338] [George Roy Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Roy_Hill),[^cite_note-339] [Lupita Nyong'o](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lupita_Nyong'o),[^cite_note-340] [Oliver Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Stone),[^cite_note-341] and [Frances McDormand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frances_McDormand).[^cite_note-342] Alumni from Yale have also made notable contributions to both music and the arts. Leading American composer from the 20th century [Charles Ives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Ives),[^cite_note-343] Broadway composer [Cole Porter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cole_Porter),[^cite_note-344] [Grammy award](44635.md) winner [David Lang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Lang_\(composer\)),[^cite_note-345] multi-[Tony Award](54741.md) winner Composer and Musicologist [Maury Yeston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maury_Yeston),[^cite_note-346] and award-winning jazz pianist and composer [Vijay Iyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vijay_Iyer)[^cite_note-347] all hail from Yale. [Hugo Boss Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Boss_Prize) winner [Matthew Barney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_Barney),[^cite_note-348] American sculptor [Richard Serra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Serra),[^cite_note-349] painter [Kehinde Wiley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kehinde_Wiley),[^cite_note-350] [MacArthur Fellows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacArthur_Fellows_Program) and contemporary artists [Tschabalala Self](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tschabalala_Self),[^cite_note-351] [Titus Kaphar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titus_Kaphar), [Richard Whitten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Whitten), and [Sarah Sze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Sze),[^cite_note-352] [Pulitzer Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulitzer_Prize_for_Editorial_Cartooning) winning cartoonist [Garry Trudeau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garry_Trudeau),[^cite_note-353] and [National Medal of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Medal_of_Arts) photorealist painter [Chuck Close](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuck_Close)[^cite_note-354] all graduated from Yale. Additional alumni include architect and [Presidential Medal of Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Medal_of_Freedom) winner [Maya Lin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_Lin),[^cite_note-355] [Pritzker Prize](53422.md) winner [Norman Foster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_Foster,_Baron_Foster_of_Thames_Bank),[^cite_note-356] and [Gateway Arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gateway_Arch) designer [Eero Saarinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eero_Saarinen).[^cite_note-357] Journalists and pundits include [Dick Cavett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cavett),[^cite_note-358] [Chris Cuomo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Cuomo),[^cite_note-359] [Anderson Cooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anderson_Cooper),[^cite_note-360] [William F. Buckley Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_F._Buckley_Jr.),[^cite_note-361] [Blake Hounshell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blake_Hounshell),[^cite_note-362] [Nia-Malika Henderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nia-Malika_Henderson)[^cite_note-363] and [Fareed Zakaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fareed_Zakaria).[^cite_note-364] Alumni distinguished in academia include literary critic and historian [Henry Louis Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Louis_Gates),[^cite_note-365] economists [Irving Fischer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irving_Fischer),[^cite_note-366] [Mahbub ul Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahbub_ul_Haq),[^cite_note-367] and Nobel Prize laureate [Paul Krugman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Krugman);[^cite_note-368] [Nobel Prize in Physics](52497.md) laureates [Ernest Lawrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Lawrence)[^cite_note-369] and [Murray Gell-Mann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murray_Gell-Mann);[^cite_note-370] Fields Medalist [John G. Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_G._Thompson);[^cite_note-371] [Human Genome Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Genome_Project) leader and [National Institutes of Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institutes_of_Health) director [Francis S. Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_S._Collins);[^cite_note-372] brain surgery pioneer [Harvey Cushing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvey_Cushing);[^cite_note-373] computer scientist [Grace Hopper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grace_Hopper);[^cite_note-374] influential mathematician and chemist [Josiah Willard Gibbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josiah_Willard_Gibbs);[^cite_note-375] [National Women's Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Women's_Hall_of_Fame) inductee and biochemist [Florence B. Seibert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florence_B._Seibert);[^cite_note-376] [Turing Award](31093.md) recipient [Ron Rivest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Rivest);[^cite_note-377] inventors [Samuel F.B. Morse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_F.B._Morse)[^cite_note-378] and [Eli Whitney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eli_Whitney);[^cite_note-379] and [Reinhold Niebuhr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reinhold_Niebuhr).[^cite_note-380]

In business, Yale has had numerous alumni and former students go on to become founders of influential business, like [William Boeing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_E._Boeing)[^cite_note-381] ([Boeing](18933266.md), [United Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Airlines)), [Briton Hadden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Briton_Hadden)[^cite_note-382] and [Henry Luce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Luce)[^cite_note-383] (*[Time Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))*), [Stephen A. Schwarzman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_A._Schwarzman)[^cite_note-384] ([Blackstone Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Blackstone_Group)), [Frederick W. Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_W._Smith)[^cite_note-385] ([FedEx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FedEx)), [Juan Trippe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Trippe)[^cite_note-386] ([Pan Am](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_Am)), [Harold Stanley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Stanley)[^cite_note-387] ([Morgan Stanley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morgan_Stanley)), [Bing Gordon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bing_Gordon)[^cite_note-388] ([Electronic Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_Arts)), and [Ben Silbermann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Silbermann)[^cite_note-389] ([Pinterest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinterest)). Other business people from Yale include former chairman and CEO of [Sears Holdings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sears_Holdings) [Edward Lampert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Lampert),[^cite_note-390] former [Time Warner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_Warner) president [Jeffrey Bewkes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Bewkes),[^cite_note-391] former [PepsiCo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PepsiCo) chairperson and CEO [Indra Nooyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indra_Nooyi),[^cite_note-392] sports agent [Donald Dell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Dell),[^cite_note-393] and investor/philanthropist Sir [John Templeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Templeton).[^cite_note-394] Yale alumni have also been the founders, petitioners, or inaugural presidents of many noted universities such as [Eleazar Wheelock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleazar_Wheelock),[^cite_note-395] the founder of [Dartmouth College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dartmouth_College); [Ezra Stiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ezra_Stiles),[^cite_note-396] petitioner of [Brown University](4157.md); the first president of [Princeton University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_University) [Jonathan Dickinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Dickinson_\(New_Jersey_minister\));[^cite_note-397] the co-founder and first president of [Cornell University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornell_University) [Andrew Dickson White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Dickson_White);[^cite_note-398] the first president of [Columbia University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_University) [Samuel Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Johnson_\(American_educator\));[^cite_note-399] founding president of the [University of California, Berkeley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_California,_Berkeley), [Henry Durant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Durant);[^cite_note-400] [Johns Hopkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johns_Hopkins_University) founding president [Daniel Coit Gilman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Coit_Gilman);[^cite_note-401] inaugural president of the [University of Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Chicago), [William Rainey Harper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rainey_Harper);[^cite_note-k496-402] and others.

Sports alumni include baseball players [Ron Darling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Darling)[^cite_note-403] and [Craig Breslow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Craig_Breslow) who in the major leagues played with fellow Yale alum [Ryan Lavarnway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryan_Lavarnway)[^cite_note-404] and baseball executives [Theo Epstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theo_Epstein)[^cite_note-405] and [George Weiss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Weiss_\(baseball\));[^cite_note-406] basketball players [Chris Dudley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Dudley), [Tony Lavelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Lavelli), [Miye Oni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miye_Oni) and [Danny Wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danny_Wolf), football players [Calvin Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvin_Hill),[^cite_note-407] [Gary Fenick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Fencik),[^cite_note-408] [Amos Alonzo Stagg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amos_Alonzo_Stagg),[^cite_note-409] and "the Father of American Football" [Walter Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Camp);[^cite_note-410] ice hockey players [Chris Higgins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Higgins_\(ice_hockey\))[^cite_note-411] and Olympian [Helen Resor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helen_Resor_\(ice_hockey\));[^cite_note-412] Olympic figure skating champions [Sarah Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Hughes)[^cite_note-413] and [Nathan Chen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathan_Chen);[^cite_note-414] nine-time [U.S. Squash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Squash) men's champion [Julian Illingworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_Illingworth);[^cite_note-415] Olympic swimmer [Don Schollander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Schollander);[^cite_note-416] Olympic rowers [Josh West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josh_West_\(rower\))[^cite_note-417] and [Rusty Wailes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rusty_Wailes);[^cite_note-418] Olympic sailor [Stuart McNay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuart_McNay);[^cite_note-419] Olympic runner [Frank Shorter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Shorter);[^cite_note-420] and others.

## In fiction and popular culture

Yale University is a cultural referent as an institution that produces some of the most elite members of society[^cite_note-isbn0-8014-3479-3-421] and its grounds, alumni, and students have been prominently portrayed in fiction and U.S. popular culture. For example, [Owen Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owen_Johnson_\(writer\))'s novel *Stover at Yale* follows the college career of Dink Stover,[^cite_note-422] and [Frank Merriwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Merriwell), the model for all later juvenile sports fiction, plays football, baseball, crew, and track at Yale while solving mysteries and righting wrongs.[^cite_note-423][^cite_note-424] In *[The Simpsons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Simpsons)*, [Charles Montgomery Burns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mr._Burns) (Mr. Burns) is a Yale alumnus and member of the secret society [Skull and Bones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skull_and_Bones); Yale and the society are featured in the episode “*[The Caper Chase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Caper_Chase)*.”[^cite_note-425] In *[Gilmore Girls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilmore_Girls)*, [Rory Gilmore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rory_Gilmore) attends Yale where she resides in [Branford College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Branford_College) and writes for the *[Yale Daily News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Daily_News)*.[^cite_note-426][^cite_note-427] Yale University also is mentioned in F. Scott Fitzgerald's novel *[The Great Gatsby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Great_Gatsby)*. The narrator, Nick Carraway, wrote a series of editorials for the *Yale News*, and Tom Buchanan was "one of the most powerful [ends](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defensive_end) that ever played football" for Yale.
